共同应对双重负担:同时应对传染病和非传染性疾病的策略

Co-managing the double burden: strategies for simultaneously tackling infectious and non-communicable diseases
作者： Jiao Li, Mei-Lan Fu, Lingyun-Fei Kong, Zi-Yu Zhao, O. E. Ohore, R. Bergquist, M. Tanner, Guo-Jing Yang, Jiao-Jiao Li, Okugbe Ebiotubo Ohore
摘要中文翻译摘要 背景 越来越多的证据表明传染病 (ID) 和非传染性疾病 (NCD) 之间存在密切的双向联系。然而,研究、卫生政策和预防实践仍然在很大程度上在单独的框架中解决这些问题。本次范围界定审查总结了关于感染性疾病和非传染性疾病之间相互关联的现有证据,并探讨了其预防和管理的综合策略。方法 本次范围界定综述系统地检索了 PubMed、Web of Science 和 Scopus,以查找 2000 年 1 月 1 日至 2026 年 4 月 20 日期间发表的英语系统综述和荟萃分析。两名评审员独立筛选符合条件的研究,并提取 ID 与 NCD 之间关联的效应估计。证据分为两个主题:传染源与癌症之间的关联,以及非传染性疾病对感染性疾病易感性或严重程度的影响。结果 在确定的 5799 份记录中,41 项荟萃分析或系统评价符合纳入标准。纳入的综述显示了病原体感染与特定癌症之间的关联,包括乙型和丙型肝炎病毒感染与肝癌、人乳头瘤病毒 感染与 宫颈癌 以及幽门螺杆菌感染与胃癌。ID 还与非传染性疾病风险或负担增加相关,结核病与慢性阻​​塞性肺病之间的关联就说明了这一点。相反,非传染性疾病可能会影响智力障碍的易感性和结果;患有糖尿病或心血管疾病的患者在感染结核病、流感或 COVID-19 后患严重疾病或不良后果的风险较高。结论 IDs 和 NCDs 可以通过共同的机制、重叠的危险因素和双向途径相互作用,强调需要将预防、早期检测、治疗 和长期管理结合起来的综合策略。在公共卫生层面,这要求卫生政策和融资机制超越特定疾病的孤岛,转向支持综合预防和控制、协调管理和跨部门行动的系统框架。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本次范围界定审查的重点是传染病(ID)和非传染性疾病(NCD)之间的相互关系,探索其预防和管理的综合策略。核心问题是如何通过共享机制、重叠的风险因素和途径来解决这些疾病类别之间的双向联系。
证据基础与方法
通过系统地搜索 PubMed、Web of Science 和 Scopus 来查找 2000 年 1 月 1 日至 2026 年 4 月 20 日期间发表的英语系统综述和荟萃分析,进行了范围界定综述。两名综述员独立筛选了合格的研究,并提取了 ID 与非传染性疾病之间关联的效应估计。
共识与结论
该综述确定了 41 项荟萃分析或系统综述,显示病原体感染与特定癌症之间的关联,例如乙型和丙型肝炎病毒与肝癌、人乳头瘤病毒 与 宫颈癌 以及幽门螺杆菌与胃癌之间的关联。ID 还与非传染性疾病负担增加有关,糖尿病或心血管疾病等非传染性疾病与结核病、流感或 COVID-19 等 ID 后出现严重后果的风险较高相关。
争议与缺口
该审查强调,尽管有证据表明感染性疾病和非传染性疾病之间存在相互关联,但研究、卫生政策和预防实践仍然主要在不同的框架中处理它们。需要更综合的方法来解决这些疾病类别之间的共同机制和重叠的风险因素。
科研与实践启示
在公共卫生层面,审查呼吁卫生政策和融资机制超越特定疾病的孤岛,转向支持综合预防和控制、协调管理和跨部门行动的系统框架。这包括将预防、早期检测、治疗 以及针对感染者和非传染性疾病的长期管理策略结合起来。

Co-managing the double burden: strategies for simultaneously tackling infectious and non-communicable diseases

Authors: Jiao Li, Mei-Lan Fu, Lingyun-Fei Kong, Zi-Yu Zhao, O. E. Ohore, R. Bergquist, M. Tanner, Guo-Jing Yang, Jiao-Jiao Li, Okugbe Ebiotubo Ohore
Original abstract (English)
Abstract Background Evidence increasingly shows close, bidirectional links between infectious diseases (IDs) and non-communicable diseases (NCDs). However, research, health policy, and prevention practices still largely address them in separate frameworks. This scoping review summarises the current evidence on the interconnections between IDs and NCDs and explores integrated strategies for their prevention and management. Methods This scoping review systematically searched PubMed, Web of Science, and Scopus for English-language systematic reviews and meta-analyses published between Jan 1, 2000, and April 20, 2026. Two reviewers independently screened eligible studies and extracted effect estimates for associations between IDs and NCDs. Evidence was grouped into two themes: associations between infectious agents and cancer, and the effects of NCDs on susceptibility to or severity of IDs. Results From 5799 records identified, 41 meta-analyses or systematic reviews met the inclusion criteria. The included reviews showed associations between pathogen infections and specific cancers, including hepatitis B and C virus infections with liver cancer, human papillomavirus infection with cervical cancer, and Helicobacter pylori infection with gastric cancer. IDs were also associated with an increased risk or burden of NCDs, as illustrated by the association between tuberculosis and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. Conversely, NCDs could affect susceptibility to and outcomes of IDs; patients with diabetes or cardiovascular disease had higher risks of severe disease or adverse outcomes following tuberculosis, influenza, or COVID-19 infection. Conclusion IDs and NCDs can interact through shared mechanisms, overlapping risk factors, and bidirectional pathways, underscoring the need for integrated strategies that combine prevention, early detection, treatment, and long-term management. At the public health level, this calls for health policies and financing mechanisms to move beyond disease-specific silos towards systemic frameworks that support integrated prevention and control, coordinated management, and cross-sectoral action.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
This scoping review focuses on the interconnections between infectious diseases (IDs) and non-communicable diseases (NCDs), exploring integrated strategies for their prevention and management. The central question is how to address the bidirectional links between these disease categories through shared mechanisms, overlapping risk factors, and pathways.
Evidence base and method
A scoping review was conducted by systematically searching PubMed, Web of Science, and Scopus for English-language systematic reviews and meta-analyses published between January 1, 2000, and April 20, 2026. Two reviewers independently screened eligible studies and extracted effect estimates for associations between IDs and NCDs.
Consensus and conclusions
The review identified 41 meta-analyses or systematic reviews showing associations between pathogen infections and specific cancers, such as hepatitis B and C viruses with liver cancer, human papillomavirus with cervical cancer, and Helicobacter pylori with gastric cancer. IDs were also linked to increased NCD burdens, and NCDs like diabetes or cardiovascular disease were associated with higher risks of severe outcomes following IDs such as tuberculosis, influenza, or COVID-19.
Controversies and gaps
The review highlights that research, health policy, and prevention practices still largely address IDs and NCDs in separate frameworks, despite evidence of their interconnections. There is a need for more integrated approaches to address the shared mechanisms and overlapping risk factors between these disease categories.
Research and practice implications
At the public health level, the review calls for health policies and financing mechanisms to move beyond disease-specific silos towards systemic frameworks that support integrated prevention and control, coordinated management, and cross-sectoral action. This includes combining prevention, early detection, treatment, and long-term management strategies for both IDs and NCDs.

基础临床双向赋能:小细胞宫颈癌分子机制与临床治疗协同突破

Basic Clinical Bidirectional Empowerment: Synergistic Breakthrough in Molecular Mechanisms and Clinical Management of Small Cell Cervical Carcinoma
作者： Mengjia Huang, Shuang Li
摘要中文翻译子宫颈小细胞癌(SCCC)是宫颈癌(CC)中罕见但高度侵袭性的亚型,具有早期侵袭、高转移性、易复发、预后极差的特点,严重损害女性的身心健康。目前,高危人乳头瘤病毒(hr-HPV,特别是HPV18)感染被认为是SCCC发生和进展的核心驱动因素之一。恶性进化不是由单一感染事件触发的,而是在多个分子水平上协同调节,包括HPV 基因组整合、关键基因突变和多个信号通路的异常激活。此外,SCCC表现出不依赖于HPV的致癌途径,主要由肿瘤蛋白53(TP53)和视网膜母细胞瘤1(RB1)中的体细胞突变介导,从而形成双重致病机制。本文基于目前的临床认识和分子机制,对SCCC的分子治疗和亚型进行综述,揭示肿瘤异质性、治疗耐药和转移之间的关联,并提出SCCC 治疗的潜在新靶点。该研究创新性地提出了基础研究与临床应用双向赋能的闭环模式。强调基础研究要面向临床实际问题,突出临床实践对基础研究的相互反馈,从而实现两个领域的动态迭代和协同突破。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述重点关注子宫颈小细胞癌 (SCCC),这是 宫颈癌 的一种罕见且具有侵袭性的亚型,检查其分子机制、临床管理和治疗靶点。核心问题围绕了解涉及高风险 人乳头瘤病毒 (hr-HPV) 和 HPV 独立致癌途径的双重致病机制。
证据基础与方法
该综述基于当前 SCCC 的临床理解和分子机制,综合了现有文献的证据。它没有指定数据库、检索日期或研究计数,但强调基础研究和临床应用相结合的闭环模型。
共识与结论
SCCC 由 hr-HPV 感染(特别是 HPV18)驱动,涉及多个分子水平的协同调节,包括 HPV 基因组 整合、基因 突变 和异常信号通路。存在双重致病机制,结合了由 TP53 和 RB1 突变 介导的 HPV 依赖性和 HPV 独立途径。
争议与缺口
该综述强调了 SCCC 发病机制 的复杂性,包括肿瘤异质性、治疗耐药性和转移,但没有明确解决相互矛盾的发现或具体的证据差距。HPV 独立途径的作用及其与 HPV 驱动机制的相互作用仍然是一个需要进一步阐明的领域。
科研与实践启示
该综述提出了 SCCC 的潜在新治疗靶点,并强调基础研究和临床实践之间动态迭代的必要性。它建议基础研究应面向临床实际问题,与临床实践相互反馈,推动协作突破。

Basic Clinical Bidirectional Empowerment: Synergistic Breakthrough in Molecular Mechanisms and Clinical Management of Small Cell Cervical Carcinoma

Authors: Mengjia Huang, Shuang Li
Original abstract (English)
Small cell carcinoma of the cervix (SCCC) is a rare yet highly aggressive subtype of cervical cancer (CC), characterized by early invasion, high metastatic potential, frequent recurrence, and extremely poor prognosis, which severely impairs women’s physical and mental health. Currently, high-risk human papillomavirus (hr-HPV, particularly HPV18) infection is recognized as one of the core drivers underlying the initiation and progression of SCCC. Malignant evolution is not triggered by a single infection event but is cooperatively regulated at multiple molecular levels, including HPV genome integration, critical gene mutations, and aberrant activation of multiple signaling pathways. In addition, SCCC exhibits an HPV-independent oncogenic pathway mainly mediated by somatic mutations in tumor protein 53 (TP53) and retinoblastoma 1 (RB1), thus forming a dual pathogenic mechanism. Based on current clinical understanding and molecular mechanisms, this paper reviews the molecular pathogenesis and subtypes of SCCC, reveals the associations between tumor heterogeneity, therapeutic resistance, and metastasis, and proposes potential novel targets for the treatment of SCCC. This study innovatively presents a closed-loop model of two-way empowerment between basic research and clinical application. It emphasizes that basic research should be oriented toward real clinical problems and highlights the reciprocal feedback of clinical practice on basic research, thereby achieving dynamic iteration and collaborative breakthroughs in both fields.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on small cell carcinoma of the cervix (SCCC), a rare and aggressive subtype of cervical cancer, examining its molecular mechanisms, clinical management, and therapeutic targets. The central question revolves around understanding the dual pathogenic mechanisms involving high-risk human papillomavirus (hr-HPV) and HPV-independent oncogenic pathways.
Evidence base and method
The review is based on current clinical understanding and molecular mechanisms of SCCC, synthesizing evidence from existing literature. It does not specify databases, search dates, or study counts but emphasizes a closed-loop model integrating basic research and clinical application.
Consensus and conclusions
SCCC is driven by hr-HPV infection (particularly HPV18) and involves cooperative regulation at multiple molecular levels, including HPV genome integration, gene mutations, and aberrant signaling pathways. A dual pathogenic mechanism exists, combining HPV-dependent and HPV-independent pathways mediated by TP53 and RB1 mutations.
Controversies and gaps
The review highlights the complexity of SCCC pathogenesis, including tumor heterogeneity, therapeutic resistance, and metastasis, but does not explicitly address conflicting findings or specific evidence gaps. The role of HPV-independent pathways and their interaction with HPV-driven mechanisms remains an area needing further elucidation.
Research and practice implications
The review proposes potential novel therapeutic targets for SCCC and emphasizes the need for dynamic iteration between basic research and clinical practice. It suggests that basic research should be oriented toward real clinical problems, with reciprocal feedback from clinical practice to drive collaborative breakthroughs.

🚨 突发动态与新闻 / Health News

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

新研究显示,女性经常选择自行收集 HPV 筛查

Women often opt for self-collection HPV screening, new studies show
新闻简报最近的两项研究强调,女性在接受 HPV 检测时强烈偏爱自行采集。在新加坡,一项针对 640 名女性的研究发现,提供自我采样选项将筛查率提高至 56.5%,而常规护理组的筛查率则为 42.8%,其中 高危型HPV 菌株的检出率更高(3.1% 对 0.3%)。同样,美国一项涉及阿片类药物 治疗 项目的 194 名患者的研究发现,90.8% 的合格参与者同意自行采集检测,其中 30 人的 HPV 检测结果呈阳性。这两项研究分别发表在《家庭医学年鉴》和《美国医学会杂志网络公开》上,得出的结论是,自检检测是可行的、可接受的,并且可以有效地扩大服务不足人群的筛查率,有可能减少医疗保健差异和 宫颈癌 死亡率。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-07-29。证据中未指定事件日期。
地点与对象
新加坡(研究 1)和美国门诊阿片类药物 治疗 计划患者(研究 2)。研究 1:640 名 30-69 岁的女性。研究 2:194 名患者,其中 174 名符合条件(89%),19 名最近筛查过,1 名不符合资格。
事件
两项研究调查了女性对 HPV 自我采样检测的反应。两项研究都发现,相比临床医生的检测,女性更喜欢自行收集。新加坡的研究发表在《家庭医学年鉴》上,美国的研究发表在《JAMA Network Open》上。
规模、影响与风险
新加坡研究:干预组56.5%的女性接受了HPV筛查,而常规护理组为42.8%。高危型HPV检测在干预组为3.1%,常规护理组为0.3%。研究:158名(90.8%)符合条件的患者同意进行自我采集检测;30人HPV检测呈阳性(7人HPV-16/18型,23人其他高危型)。
应对、状态与不确定性
新加坡研究:在临床医生采样的同时提供自我采样可显着提高筛查率和 高危型HPV 检测率。研究:自我收集测试是可行且可接受的,患者资格和采用率较高。30 名检测呈阳性的患者中,有 16 名患者得到了后续护理。研究表明,自助收集可以提高服务不足人群的筛查率。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Women often opt for self-collection HPV screening, new studies show

News Brief
Two recent studies highlight women's strong preference for self-collection HPV testing when offered. In Singapore, a study of 640 women found that providing self-sampling options increased screening uptake to 56.5% compared to 42.8% in the usual-care group, with a higher detection rate of high-risk HPV strains (3.1% vs. 0.3%). Similarly, a U.S. study involving 194 patients in an opioid treatment program found that 90.8% of eligible participants agreed to self-collection testing, with 30 testing positive for HPV. Both studies, published in the Annals of Family Medicine and JAMA Network Open respectively, conclude that self-collection testing is feasible, acceptable, and effective at expanding screening rates in underserved populations, potentially reducing healthcare disparities and cervical cancer mortality.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Main navigation News Antimicrobial Resistance COVID-19 Chronic Wasting Disease Influenza Public Health Alerts All Topics A-Z Coronavirus Vaccines Roadmap Influenza Vaccines Roadmap Roadmaps for Priority Pathogens Vaccine Integrity Project CIDRAP Leadership Forum . . Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search CLOSE Search Support Menu Women often opt for self-collection HPV screening, new studies show Meghan Holohan Human Papillomavirus (HPV) Rawpixel / iStock Share Copied to clipboard Screening for human papillomavirus (HPV) remains an effective way to find high-risk strains of HPV early. With self-sample HPV testing kits, women can collect their own swab instead of having a doctor or nurse conduct the test. Two studies examine how women respond to HPV self-sampling testing and find that, when it’s offered, women prefer it. The self-collecting tests could encourage more HPV screening in populations missed in annual screenings. Self-collection could improve screening rates... In the first study, researchers randomly assigned 640 women ages 30 to 69 in Singapore to one of two equal-sized groups. Healthcare providers asked women in the regular-care group if they would undergo clinician testing for HPV. In the interventional group, medical staff first asked if women would like a clinician to screen them for HPV. If they declined, they then offered them a self-collection kit. The study was published this week in the Annals of Family Medicine. Scientists found a higher rate of high-risk HPV strains, 3.1%, in the interventional cohort, compared with 0.3% in the usual-care group. What’s more, women offered either clinician testing or self-testing were more likely to agree to screening, with 56.5% undergoing HPV screening in the intervention group and 42.8% in the usual-care group. “Offering self-sampling HPV DNA testing alongside clinician sampling significantly increased screening uptake and high-risk HPV detection rates,” wrote the authors, led by scientists at the National Healthcare Group in Singapore. The increase in willingness to undergo self-testing for HPV could have a positive impact in the country, where only 43% of women are screened annually for the virus, which is much lower than the national goal of screening 70% of eligible women. ...and testing rates for women with substance abuse A study published today in JAMA Network Open examined the willingness of women with substance use disorder to engage in self-collection HPV testing. Such women tend to avoid medical care and are less likely to receive regular HPV screening. They’re also at increased risk of having long-term HPV infections. HPV self-collection testing can expand cervical cancer screening in settings that do not currently offer this service and reach previously unscreened patients, which should play a role in reduced health care disparities and cervical cancer mortality. Scientists at the University of Washington wondered if offering self-testing at an outpatient opioid treatment program would increase women’s willingness to undergo HPV screening. Of 194 patients, 174 (89%) qualified for the study, 19 (9.8%) had recently been screened, and one (0.5%) was ineligible because she didn’t have a cervix. The vast majority of patients, 158 (90.8%), agreed to undergo self-collection HPV testing, which they conducted in the opioid treatment program’s restroom. Of those, 128 (81%) did not test positive for HPV; seven (4.4%) tested positive for HPV-16 or HPV-18, both high-risk strains of the virus; and 23 (14.6%) tested positive for another high-risk HPV strain. Of the 30 patients with positive HPV tests, 16 participated in follow-up care. “Using HPV self-collection testing was feasible and acceptable, with both high patient eligibility and uptake,” the authors wrote. “HPV self-collection testing can expand cervical cancer screening in settings that do not currently offer this service and reach previously unscreened patients, which should play a role in reduced health care disparities and cervical cancer mortality.” Related news Previous Next Slight gains made in global childhood vaccination rates, says new report Meghan Holohan HIV dramatically increases the risk of HPV-related anal cancer, data show Liz Szabo Some patients opt for at-home cervical screening for HPV, but more outreach is needed, study finds Meghan Holohan HPV vaccination eliminated cervical cancer deaths in young UK women in recent years, study suggests Meghan Holohan ‘Everyone who has a cervix is at risk’: Transgender people often miss out on cervical cancer screenings Liz Szabo HPV vaccines allow nations, states to set deadline for eliminating cervical cancer Liz Szabo ‘Something I’d never heard of almost killed me’: Men face rising threat of HPV-related cancers Liz Szabo Two sisters, one virus: A family devastated by HPV Liz Szabo This week's top reads FDA identifies new Cyclospora outbreak with unknown source The trace-back investigation for the new outbreak is underway as cases mount across the country. 自我采样增强 HPV 检测并提高 宫颈癌 筛查率 - Bioengineer.org

Self-Sampling Boosts HPV Detection and Increases Cervical Cancer Screening Rates - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报新加坡的一项务实随机对照试验表明,提供自行收集的 HPV 样本以及临床医生收集的样本可显着提高 宫颈癌 筛查率和 HPV 检测。在 650 名 30-69 岁的女性中,干预组的筛查率达到 56.6%(常规护理为 42.8%),HPV 检测率为 3.1%(0.3%)。值得注意的是,十个 高危型HPV 病例中有七个是以前未经筛查的女性,其中五个是通过自我采样发现的。这表明自我抽样在不影响 高危型HPV 识别的情况下降低了障碍,尽管教育程度较低的女性仍然存在公平问题。研究结果支持以患者为中心的策略,以提高首次和持续筛查的参与度。

发表日期:2026 年 7 月 27 日。活动期间:未明确说明,但试验在新加坡进行,结果于 2026 年报告。

新加坡的一项实用随机对照试验测试了提供自行收集的 HPV 样本以及临床医生收集的样本是否会提高 宫颈癌 筛查参与度和 HPV 检出率。干预组首先接受临床医生采样,如果拒绝则自行采样,而常规护理组仅接受临床医生采样。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Self-Sampling Boosts HPV Detection and Increases Cervical Cancer Screening Rates - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A pragmatic randomized controlled trial in Singapore demonstrated that offering self-collected HPV samples alongside clinician-collected specimens significantly improved cervical cancer screening rates and HPV detection. Among 650 women aged 30-69, the intervention group achieved 56.6% screening uptake (vs. 42.8% in usual care) and 3.1% HPV detection (vs. 0.3%). Notably, seven of ten high-risk HPV cases were in previously unscreened women, with five detected via self-sampling. This suggests self-sampling lowers barriers without compromising high-risk HPV identification, though equity concerns persist for women with lower education. The findings support patient-centered strategies to improve both first-time and ongoing screening participation.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, July 31, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Cancer Self-Sampling Boosts HPV Detection and Increases Cervical Cancer Screening Rates by Bioengineer July 27, 2026 in Cancer Reading Time: 2 mins read 0 Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A pragmatic randomized controlled trial in Singapore tested whether cervical cancer screening participation and human papillomavirus (HPV) detection could be improved by offering self-collected samples alongside clinician-collected specimens. The study specifically targeted women aged 30 to 69 who were due for screening, addressing a persistent challenge: many eligible individuals decline clinic-based collection. Researchers enrolled 650 women and randomly assigned them to either an intervention arm or usual care. Each arm included 325 participants. In the intervention group, women were informed about both sampling routes and were offered clinician sampling first, with self-sampling provided if they declined. The usual-care group was offered clinician sampling only, establishing a direct comparison between patient choice and clinician-only collection. Discover more Medical Tests & Exams cancer Hospitals & Treatment Centers Marketing HPV detection rates were substantially higher in the intervention arm, rising to 3.1% compared with 0.3% in the usual-care arm. Screening uptake also increased meaningfully, reaching 56.6% versus 42.8%. These two outcomes together suggest that self-sampling lowered practical and behavioral barriers without compromising the ability to identify high-risk HPV types. The investigators reported that the higher detection rate was driven largely by increased participation among previously unscreened women. Only a subset of participants contributed to the observed high-risk HPV cases, but among the ten cases identified, seven came from women who had not been screened before—an especially important signal for public health targeting under-screened populations. Among the newly engaged women with high-risk HPV, detection frequently depended on the self-sampling option. Five of the seven cases among previously unscreened participants were detected through self-collected samples, highlighting the role of modality choice in reaching those most at risk of missed opportunities. Notably, uptake rose not only among women with no prior screening history but also among those with a regular screening pattern. This indicates that self-sampling may improve both first-time engagement and continuity, rather than benefiting only those completely outside screening programs. Within the intervention arm, among participants who completed screening, 56.4% selected self-sampling. Preferences were not uniform: women with lower educational attainment were less likely to choose self-sampling and more likely to forego screening entirely, pointing to equity-sensitive design needs. Discover more Health Foundations & Medical Research Social Sciences Books & Literature Economics OBGYN Overall, the trial supports patient-centered screening strategies that present self-sampling as an accessible alternative while maintaining clinician involvement as an initial step. For viral science news, the finding is straightforward: better uptake means more HPV cases are found earlier, strengthening the upstream chain that prevents cervical cancer. Cervical Cancer From HPV : A Preventable Disease - โรงพยาบาลรามคําแหง

News Brief
Cervical cancer is the most common cancer among women in Thailand, with over 6,000 new cases and 2,600 deaths annually. Human Papillomavirus (HPV) is the primary cause, detected in 99.7% of cases. While conventional Pap smears have limitations, advanced HPV DNA testing offers high sensitivity (90%) for early detection. Ramkhamhaeng Hospital provides PCR-based HPV testing, combining sample collection during pelvic exams with results within seven days. Early intervention through regular screening can prevent progression, as precancerous lesions take years to develop. Women aged 30+ are advised to undergo combined Pap smear and HPV testing every three years if normal, or more frequently if inconclusive. Prevention remains key, with early detection being the most effective strategy.
Original source text (English)
Cervical Cancer From HPV : A Preventable Disease Cervical cancer is the second most common cancer among women worldwide. However, in Thailand, it ranks as the most frequently diagnosed cancer among women, particularly in those aged 35–60. Each year, over 6,000 new cases are reported, with approximately 2,600 fatalities. Nevertheless, cervical cancer is a preventable disease if abnormalities are detected early through regular medical screenings. HPV: The Leading Cause of Cervical Cancer Extensive research has confirmed that the Human Papillomavirus (HPV) is the primary cause of cervical cancer. Studies show that HPV is present in nearly all cervical cancer cases (99.7%). This virus is transmitted through sexual contact and is the same virus responsible for genital warts. Different HPV Strains and Their Risks There are over 200 strains of HPV that can infect the genital area, with at least 13 identified as high-risk strains for cervical cancer. These high-risk strains can cause abnormal cell changes in the cervix, which may develop into cancer over time. Low-risk strains, on the other hand, primarily cause genital warts without leading to cancer. Cervical Cancer Screening The Pap smear test, widely recognized as an effective method for detecting abnormal cervical cells, has significantly reduced cervical cancer incidence worldwide. However, conventional Pap smear methods, including liquid-based cytology techniques such as ThinPrep and Liquid Prep, have limitations in sensitivity. False-negative results may occur, where abnormal cervical cells are present but undetected, delaying early treatment for cervical cancer. Advanced HPV DNA Testing HPV DNA testing is a molecular diagnostic technique with high sensitivity (approximately 90%). This method can detect HPV infections in their earliest stages, even before cellular abnormalities appear. When combined with a Pap smear, HPV DNA testing enhances the accuracy of cervical cancer screening and improves early detection rates. Since precancerous lesions take years to develop into cancer, early detection allows timely intervention, significantly reducing the risk of progression. Maintaining overall health, including mental well-being, also plays a crucial role in helping the body clear HPV infections naturally. Who Should Undergo HPV Testing with a Pap Smear? The American Cancer Society and the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) recommend HPV testing alongside a Pap smear for women aged 30 and above. If results are normal, screening can be performed every three years. However, if the results are inconclusive—for instance, a positive HPV test but no abnormal cells in the Pap smear—repeat testing every 6–12 months is advised for further monitoring. HPV Testing, Ramkhamhaeng Hospital Women can undergo HPV testing using the Polymerase Chain Reaction (PCR) method at the Obstetrics and Gynecology Clinic, Ramkhamhaeng Hospital, under the supervision of experienced obstetricians and gynecologists. Sample Collection and Testing The sample for HPV testing is collected from the cervix during a routine pelvic exam, along with a Pap smear. The sample is then sent to a specialized laboratory for analysis, with results available within seven days. Cervical cancer is a preventable disease, and medical screenings can detect abnormalities in the pre-cancerous stage before symptoms appear. Early detection remains the most effective way to prevent. Last updated: August 25, 2021
View five news elements
Time
Published on July 27, 2026; event context not specified in evidence.
Location and population
Thailand; women aged 35–60, particularly affected by cervical cancer.
Event
Confirmed: Cervical cancer is the most frequently diagnosed cancer among women in Thailand, with over 6,000 new cases and approximately 2,600 fatalities reported annually. The disease is preventable through early detection via medical screenings.
Scale, impact and risk
Over 6,000 new cases annually; approximately 2,600 fatalities. HPV is present in 99.7% of cervical cancer cases. High-risk HPV strains can cause abnormal cell changes leading to cancer over time.
Response, status and uncertainty
Advanced HPV DNA testing (90% sensitivity) is available at Ramkhamhaeng Hospital's Obstetrics and Gynecology Clinic using PCR. Early detection through screening (Pap smear combined with HPV testing) can prevent progression. Sample collection occurs during pelvic exams, with results in seven days. Last updated: August 25, 2021.

德里 HC 在安全请求下让 HPV 疫苗 继续行驶

Delhi HC Lets HPV Vaccine Drive Continue Amid Safety Plea
新闻简报The Delhi High Court has allowed India’s HPV vaccination campaign to proceed after declining an interim stay, citing no immediate harm to justify suspension. The court, however, emphasized the need for further examination of informed consent, safety evidence, and monitoring protocols. The nationwide campaign, targeting adolescent girls aged 14, has already administered over 55 lakh doses, with approximately 120 minor adverse events reported. The petitioners, including a gynecologist and health entrepreneur, raised concerns about bodily autonomy, informed consent, and potential pressure on health workers. The government defended the vaccine’s global safety record, citing use in 160 countries. The court has sought detailed responses from health authorities while the vaccination drive continues.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026 年 7 月 31 日;事件发生于2026年7月29日,疫苗接种活动于2026年2月28日启动。
地点与对象
印度,全国性活动针对14岁的少女,在全国36个邦和联邦地区中约有1.2千万名符合条件的女孩被确定。
事件
德里高等法院拒绝临时中止印度的 HPV 疫苗接种 活动,允许该活动继续进行,同时有关知情同意、安全证据和监测的问题仍在审查中。
规模、影响与风险
Over 55 lakh doses administered in India; approximately 120 recipients reported minor events such as headache or nausea; no immediate harm demonstrated to justify suspension; campaign targets cervical cancer prevention.
应对、状态与不确定性
法院指示卫生和家庭福利部、印度医学研究委员会和中央药品标准控制组织提交详细答复;疫苗接种 继续进行,而法律挑战尚未决;安全和同意程序仍在审查中。

Delhi HC Lets HPV Vaccine Drive Continue Amid Safety Plea

News Brief
The Delhi High Court has allowed India’s HPV vaccination campaign to proceed after declining an interim stay, citing no immediate harm to justify suspension. The court, however, emphasized the need for further examination of informed consent, safety evidence, and monitoring protocols. The nationwide campaign, targeting adolescent girls aged 14, has already administered over 55 lakh doses, with approximately 120 minor adverse events reported. The petitioners, including a gynecologist and health entrepreneur, raised concerns about bodily autonomy, informed consent, and potential pressure on health workers. The government defended the vaccine’s global safety record, citing use in 160 countries. The court has sought detailed responses from health authorities while the vaccination drive continues.
Original source text (English)
MEDINDIA About About Us Editorial Team Exclusive Interviews In the News Newsletters Partners & Affiliates Advertise With Us Contact Us Press Sitemap Feedback EN English हिन्दी Español français 中文 LOGIN REGISTER Explore Healthy Living News Health A-Z Calculators Articles Drugs Directories Education More Loading... MEDINDIA MEDINDIA Consult Online Doctor Courses Explore Healthy Living News Health A-Z Calculators Articles Drugs Directories Education More Search Medindia Medindia » News » Drug News » Delhi HC Lets HPV Vaccine Drive Continue Amid Safety Plea Delhi HC Lets HPV Vaccine Drive Continue Amid Safety Plea by Shravanthi Vikram on Jul 31 2026 1:29 PM Listen to this article 0:00/0:00 The Delhi High Court has allowed India’s HPV vaccination campaign to continue while keeping questions about informed consent, safety evidence and monitoring open for further examination. India’s nationwide human papillomavirus vaccination campaign will continue after the Delhi High Court declined to grant an interim stay sought through a public interest litigation. The petition challenges the rollout of the HPV vaccine for adolescent girls and raises questions about long-term safety, scientific justification, informed parental consent and the monitoring of adverse events following immunization. A Division Bench comprising Chief Justice D.K. Upadhyaya and Justice Tejas Karia found no sufficient basis to stop the campaign at this stage. Reports on the July 29 hearing said the court observed that no “immediate harm” had been demonstrated to justify an interim suspension. However, the Bench did not dismiss the petition or deliver a final ruling on the programme’s safety or legality. It directed the Center and relevant research, regulatory and immunization bodies to file detailed replies explaining the justification for introducing the vaccine. Tamil Nadu Paves the Way for a Cancer-Free Future With Free HPV Vaccines Tamil Nadu, India, has officially taken a historic step towards the eradication of cervical cancer by initiating a free HPV vaccination drive for girls aged 1-14 years. Interim Order Is Not a Final Endorsement The decision allows vaccination to continue while the legal challenge remains pending. The court’s refusal to grant an interim stay should not be interpreted as a final judicial declaration that every concern raised in the petition is unfounded. The scientific evidence supporting the programme, its consent procedures and its adverse-event reporting system will remain under examination during subsequent hearings. At an earlier hearing on July 22, the Bench described the questions raised by the petition as important because the campaign concerns adolescent girls across the country. The court said it was not questioning the government’s intentions but stressed that reports of adverse events required careful evaluation. It consequently sought responses from the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, the Indian Council of Medical Research and the Central Drugs Standard Control Organization. Cervical Screening Will Only be Needed Three Times With HPV Vaccine Now with the HPV vaccine, screening will only be needed three times in women. The new programme called HPV primary testing is set to be introduced in England by December 2019. Petition Raises Consent and Monitoring Concerns The PIL was filed by gynecologist Dr Sujata Mittal and health entrepreneur Jitendra Chouksey. The petitioners allege that the vaccination programme may affect the rights of adolescent girls to bodily autonomy, personal liberty and informed consent. They have cited Articles 19 and 21 of the Constitution while seeking greater transparency around the campaign. The plea claims that vaccination is being facilitated through schools and public health facilities without consistently obtaining adequate informed parental consent. It also alleges that frontline health workers have been given enrolment targets, which could create pressure to secure participation. These remain allegations made by the petitioners and have not been accepted as established findings by the court. The petition additionally refers to individual medical events reported after vaccination and asks the government to disclose detailed safety and causality-assessment data. An adverse event following immunization, or AEFI, is any medical occurrence reported after vaccination. Its timing alone does not prove that the vaccine caused the event; a formal investigation is required to determine whether a causal relationship exists. New HPV Vaccine Reduces The Risk Of HPV-Related Diseases A new improved Human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine called 9vHPV has been developed greatly reduces the risk of HPV infection and HPV-related diseases. Center Defends HPV Vaccination Programme Representing the Union government, Additional Solicitor General Chetan Sharma reportedly told the court that HPV vaccines have been administered to more than 55 crore people worldwide and are used in nearly 160 countries.(1✔ ✔Trusted Source Delhi HC declines to stay HPV vaccination drive, seeks Centreâ€TMs reply Go to source) The Center also submitted that more than 55 lakh doses had been given in India. Around 120 recipients reportedly experienced minor events such as headache or nausea, the court was told. These figures were presented by the government during the hearing and will form part of the material examined as the case continues. The court has asked the authorities to provide separate and detailed responses rather than relying only on oral submissions. Official government data show that India launched the nationwide HPV vaccination campaign on February 28, 2026. Approximately 1.2 crore girls aged 14 were identified as eligible across all 36 states and Union Territories. Vaccination is provided free at designated government facilities, including primary and community health centers, district hospitals and government medical colleges. HPV Vaccine Dose Reduced to Two Shots for Cervical Cancer : CDC Recommends Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends two dose -HPV vaccine schedule for preventing cervical cancer and infections in preteens. HPV Vaccination Targets Cervical Cancer Human papillomavirus is a group of approximately 200 viruses transmitted mainly through intimate skin-to-skin and sexual contact. Most HPV infections disappear without treatment. However, persistent infection with certain high-risk types can cause abnormal cellular changes and eventually lead to cancer. Almost all cervical cancer cases are linked to high-risk HPV infection. HPV is also associated with cancers affecting the anus, vulva, vagina, penis and mouth or throat.(2✔ ✔Trusted Source Human papillomavirus and cancer Go to source) HPV types 16 and 18 are responsible for approximately 70% of cervical cancers. Vaccines are most effective when administered before exposure to the virus. The World Health Organization recommends HPV vaccination as a priority for girls aged nine to 14. Depending on age and immune status, the vaccine may be administered in one or two doses, while immunocompromised individuals may need additional doses. Vaccination prevents new infections but does not treat an existing HPV infection. Cervical screening remains necessary later in life because no vaccine protects against every cancer-causing HPV type. Safety Evidence and Surveillance Remain Important The WHO’s Global Advisory Committee on Vaccine Safety has repeatedly reviewed data on HPV vaccines and considers them to have a strong safety profile.(1✔ ✔Trusted Source Delhi HC declines to stay HPV vaccination drive, seeks Centreâ€TMs reply Go to source) Common short-term reactions to vaccination may include pain or swelling at the injection site, headache, dizziness, nausea or fainting. Severe allergic reactions are possible but rare.(1✔ ✔Trusted Source Delhi HC declines to stay HPV vaccination drive, seeks Centreâ€TMs reply Go to source) Large safety reviews have not established causal links between HPV vaccination and several long-term conditions that have been attributed to the vaccine in case reports. Established global safety evidence does not remove the need for transparent monitoring. A national campaign must still provide clear information, obtain appropriate consent, investigate serious medical events and publish safety findings in a way that allows independent assessment. The Delhi High Court will continue examining whether India’s rollout meets these requirements while the vaccination programme remains in operation. Seegene 利用大规模临床证据推进全面的 HPV 和 STI PCR 检测 - BioPharma APAC

Seegene Advances Comprehensive HPV and STI PCR Testing with Large-Scale Clinical Evidence - BioPharma APAC
新闻简报Seegene宣布了一种基于综合征PCR的生殖道感染综合检测方法,可同时检测性传播感染(STIs)和人乳头瘤病毒(HPV)。根据42个月内收集的60,000份检测结果分析,该公司发现HPV阳性病例中有82%存在STI病原体,STI阳性病例中有46%存在HPV,阳性病例中有71%为共同检测。这些发现支持综合检测在宫颈癌风险评估和生殖健康诊断中的潜在价值。该公告紧随Seegene于2026年7月8日启动的全球百万临床研究之后,该研究旨在通过积累全球医疗机构的真实世界临床数据,为PCR检测的新全球标准提供科学依据。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布日期:2026年7月28日。活动日期:2026年7月29日(公告日期)。分析周期:42个月(测试数据收集)。
地点与对象
全球医疗保健机构(通过全球百万临床研究)。受影响人群:接受 HPV 和 STI 检测的个人,重点关注生殖道感染和 宫颈癌 风险评估。
事件
Seegene 宣布推出一种基于综合征 PCR 的生殖道感染 (RTI) 综合检测方法,可同时检测 STI 和 HPV。在此之前,全球百万临床研究于 2026 年 7 月 8 日启动。该公告基于对 60,000 项检测结果的分析,并作为支持 PCR 检测全球新标准的科学证据。
规模、影响与风险
对60,000份检测结果的分析显示,HPV阳性病例中有82%检测到STI病原体,STI阳性病例中有46%检测到HPV,阳性病例中有71%为共同检测。这些发现支持将STI检测与HPV筛查同时进行,以识别无症状STI、评估合并感染和支持生殖健康评估。未明确说明风险级别。
应对、状态与不确定性
Seegene 正在通过全球百万临床研究验证综合 HPV 和 STI 检测的临床效用,使用 STAgoraTM 进行实际数据积累和统计评估。该倡议旨在为新的全球 PCR 检测标准建立科学证据。该分析基于公司内部数据,代表了初步结果,需要通过正在进行的研究进一步验证。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Seegene Advances Comprehensive HPV and STI PCR Testing with Large-Scale Clinical Evidence - BioPharma APAC

News Brief
Seegene has announced a comprehensive syndromic PCR-based testing approach for reproductive tract infections that simultaneously detects sexually transmitted infections (STIs) and human papillomavirus (HPV). Based on analysis of 60,000 test results collected over 42 months, the company found that STI pathogens were present in 82% of HPV-positive cases, HPV in 46% of STI-positive cases, and co-detection in 71% of positive cases. These findings support the potential value of comprehensive testing for cervical cancer risk assessment and reproductive health diagnostics. The announcement follows Seegene's launch of the Global Million Clinical Study on July 8, 2026, which aims to generate scientific evidence for a new global standard of PCR testing by accumulating real-world clinical data from healthcare institutions worldwide.
Original source text (English)
01 August, 2026 | Saturday BioPharma America BioPharma Europe BioPharma Middle East BioPharma Africa BioPharma APAC Events Media Pack Login BioPharma Pharma & Biopharma Manufacturing Pharma Research Pharma QC Clinical Trials Drug Discovery Drug Approval BioSimilar Pharma Packaging LAB LAB Business LAB Health & Safety LAB Products LAB Design LAB Testing LifeScience Biotech Biologics BioSimilar Clinical & Diagnostic Emerging Biotech Vaccines BioChemicals Cell & Gene Antibody Drug Conjugates Contract Services Contract Research Clinical Research Contract Manufacturing Methods Development Pre-Clinical Outsourcing Bio Digital Digital Health Digital CRO’s Health CIO Digital Innovation Artificial Intelligence Patent Medtech Med’s Industry Medical Devices Medical Diagnostic Bio Marketing Bio Jobs Bio Special WhitePaper Product in Action Premium Article BioInvest Bio Informatics Webinar MasterControl Resource Centre Special Report Sustainable Biopharma Mergers and Acquisitions Bio Insight Bio Analysis Bio Feature Bio Reports Bio-Interaction BioResources Consumer Health Healthcare FemHealth Focus Endocrinology Neurology Cancer / Immunology Cancer / Oncology COVID19 Mental Health Diabetes Nutraceutical & Supplement Medicine Countries Australia New Zealand South Korea Japan Malaysia Thailand Singapore Indonesia India China Taiwan Hong Kong Vietnam SAARC Countries SEA Countries EMEA (Middle East & Africa) North America Europe World LifeScience Biologics Seegene Advances Comprehensive HPV and STI PCR Testing with Large-Scale Clinical Evidence Seegene Advances Comprehensive HPV and STI PCR Testing with Large-Scale Clinical Evidence 29 July 2026 | Wednesday | News Analysis of 60,000 test results found STI pathogens in 82% of HPV-positive cases, supporting a syndromic testing approach to strengthen cervical cancer risk assessment and reproductive health diagnostics. STAgoraTM analysis of approximately 60,000 test results collected over 42 months showed that STI pathogens were detected in 82% of HPV-positive cases Findings support the potential value of a comprehensive testing approach that provides both HPV and STI pathogen information Seegene to validate the clinical utility of comprehensive HPV & STI testing through the Global Million Clinical Study and establish scientific evidence for new global standards of PCR testing Seegene Inc., a global molecular diagnostics company, announced a comprehensive syndromic PCR-based testing approach for reproductive tract infections (RTIs) through the simultaneous detection of sexually transmitted infections (STIs) and human papillomavirus (HPV). The announcement follows Seegene's launch of the Global Million Clinical Study (GMCS) on July 8. The initiative aims to generate scientific evidence for Global New Standard of PCR testing by accumulating real-world clinical data from healthcare institutions worldwide and evaluating the clinical utility of disease-specific diagnostic testing strategies. HPV is widely recognized as the primary cause of cervical cancer, and certain high-risk genotypes, particularly HPV-16 and HPV-18, are closely associated with disease progression. Because screening and follow-up strategies may vary depending on the HPV genotype detected, identifying not only the presence of HPV but also the genotype provides important clinical information for patient management. STIs are caused by a variety of pathogens, including Chlamydia trachomatis and Neisseria gonorrhoeae. They may present with a broad range of symptoms, including abnormal discharge, dysuria, genital pain or itching, dyspareunia, lower abdominal pain, genital ulcers, vesicles or warts, and infertility. Because different pathogens often cause similar clinical symptoms, identifying the causative pathogen with symptoms alone may be difficult. In addition, asymptomatic infections are common and may contribute to unrecognized transmission. Recent studies have suggested that STIs and vaginal dysbiosis may be associated with changes in the local inflammatory, immune, and microbial environment of the cervix, potentially influencing HPV acquisition, persistence, or clearance. This growing body of evidence underscores the importance of interpreting HPV and STI findings within the broader clinical context – including the patient's age, symptoms, sexual history and exposure risk, pregnancy plan, prior infection history and the specific organism detected. Together, these findings provide a scientific rationale for further evaluating a comprehensive HPV and STI testing approach that assesses HPV risk alongside concurrent sexually transmitted and reproductive tract infections. Using STAgoraTM, Seegene's real-time test data analytics and statistical platform, the company internally analyzed approximately 60,000 HPV & STI co-testing results generated over a 42-month period. The analysis showed that STI pathogens were detected in 82% of HPV-positive cases, while HPV was detected in 46% of STI-positive cases. In addition, co-detection of HPV and STI pathogens was observed in 71% of positive cases identified through comprehensive HPV & STI testing. These findings demonstrate the potential application of concurrent STI testing with HPV screening. The promising applications may include identifying asymptomatic STIs, assessing co-infections in HPV-positive individuals, and supporting reproductive health evaluations during pregnancy planning or infertility assessment. Through GMCS, Seegene plans to continue accumulating clinical evidence on comprehensive HPV & STI testing and to statistically evaluate its clinical utility and applicability using STAgoraTM. HPV 疫苗接种:今天保护女孩免受明天的 宫颈癌 - standardmedia.co.ke

HPV vaccination: Protecting girls today from cervical cancer tomorrow - standardmedia.co.ke
新闻简报肯尼亚面临着巨大的 宫颈癌 负担,每年约有 5,845 例新病例和 3,591 例死亡。为了解决这个问题,该国已将 HPV 疫苗接种 作为一项关键的预防措施,针对可能接触该病毒之前的 9 至 14 岁女孩。2025 年 11 月,肯尼亚从两剂 HPV 疫苗接种 方案过渡到单剂 HPV 疫苗接种 方案,符合世卫组织关于提高可及性并减少社区障碍的建议。虽然 疫苗接种 至关重要,但它并不能取代 宫颈癌 筛查,宫颈癌 筛查对于 25-49 岁女性的早期检测仍然至关重要。这种综合方法旨在通过将疫苗接种与定期筛查和癌前病变的早期治疗相结合来减少宫颈癌、发病率和死亡率。

肯尼亚;重点关注 9-14 岁的女孩,将其作为 HPV 疫苗接种 的主要目标群体,重点覆盖面临医疗保健获取障碍的社区。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

HPV vaccination: Protecting girls today from cervical cancer tomorrow - standardmedia.co.ke

News Brief
Kenya faces a significant burden of cervical cancer, with approximately 5,845 new cases and 3,591 deaths annually. To address this, the country has implemented HPV vaccination as a key preventive measure, targeting girls aged 9-14 before potential exposure to the virus. In November 2025, Kenya transitioned from a two-dose to a single-dose HPV vaccination schedule, aligning with WHO recommendations to improve accessibility and reduce barriers in communities. While vaccination is crucial, it is not a replacement for cervical cancer screening, which remains essential for early detection in women aged 25-49. This integrated approach aims to reduce cervical cancer incidence and mortality by combining vaccination with regular screening and early treatment of precancerous lesions.

ESTATE DIGGER TRIBUTES LIVE: KTN LIVE: Radio Maisha LIVE: Spice FM LIVE: Berur FM HPV vaccination: Protecting girls today from cervical cancer tomorrow Health Opinion By Marleen Temmerman | 5d ago | 4 min read Corporate media solutions Cervical cancer is among the leading cancers affecting women in Kenya. [Courtesy] Vaccines & Immunizations Cervical cancer remains one of the clearest examples of a disease that modern medicine knows how to prevent, yet too many women continue to die from it. Globally, about 660,000 women were diagnosed with cervical cancer in 2022, and roughly 350,000 died from the disease. The burden is heaviest in low- and middle-income countries, where access to vaccination, screening, diagnosis and treatment is often limited. Kenya is no exception. Cervical cancer is among the leading cancers affecting women in the country. Recent Ministry of Health figures, citing GLOBOCAN 2022, estimate about 5,845 new cervical cancer cases and 3,591 deaths in Kenya each year. That means thousands of families continue to lose mothers, daughters, sisters, wives, colleagues and friends to a cancer that can often be prevented. This is why the conversation around the human papillomavirus, better known as HPV, deserves attention. The concern is not merely about a vaccine. It is about preventing cancer before it starts. HPV is a very common virus, transmitted mainly through sexual contact. Most HPV infections clear naturally and never cause disease. The problem arises when infection with high-risk HPV types persists for years. Over time, this can cause abnormal changes in the cells of the cervix. If those changes are not detected and treated early, they may develop into cervical cancer. Persistent infection with high-risk HPV causes virtually all cervical cancers, with HPV types 16 and 18 responsible for a large share of cases worldwide. Cancer That is where vaccination comes in. The HPV vaccine is designed to protect against the high-risk HPV types that cause most cervical cancers. It also helps prevent some other HPV-related cancers, including cancers of the vulva, vagina, anus and oropharynx. Importantly, the vaccine works best when given before exposure to HPV, which is why health authorities recommend vaccination in early adolescence. WHO identifies girls aged 9 to 14 as the primary target group for HPV vaccination, before they become sexually active. Kenya introduced HPV vaccination into the national immunisation programme in 2019, initially targeting 10-year-old girls. In November 2025, the country moved from a two-dose to a single-dose HPV vaccination schedule, in line with WHO recommendations and global evidence supporting simplified delivery. This shift is important because it reduces the need for repeat clinic visits and can help reach more girls, especially in communities where distance, transport costs and time away from work or school are real barriers. Some parents understandably ask: why vaccinate girls so young? The answer is simple. Prevention must come before exposure. We do not wait for children to encounter measles before vaccinating them against measles. In the same way, HPV vaccination is offered early so that girls are protected long before they face the risk of infection. People & Society The vaccine should also not be misunderstood as a replacement for screening. It is one part of a wider cervical cancer prevention strategy. Women who are already sexually active, including those who were not vaccinated as children, still benefit greatly from cervical cancer screening. Screening helps detect HPV infection or precancerous lesions before they become cancer. These early changes are not cancer, and when found in time, they can be treated. . Keep Reading Health practitioners call for inclusivity in policy formulation How one father is leading Kenya's malaria fight State rolls out five-year plan to fight cervical cancer Pharmacy-based PrEP emerges as key in Kenya's HIV fight In Kenya, national screening guidelines recommend cervical cancer screening for women aged between 25 and 49. HPV testing is recommended as the primary screening method for women above 30 where available, while visual inspection with acetic acid, visual inspection with Lugol’s iodine and Pap smear testing may be used depending on resources and clinical circumstances. Health Conditions The good news is that the development of cervical cancer from the time of infection usually takes over 10 years. This is plenty of time to screen women who are sexually active and find out if they are carrying the virus or if they have developed precancerous lesions in the cervix, that can easily be treated, hence preventing cancer in those who have been exposed already. Screening can be done via a vaginal swab for HPV, obtained by a healthcare provider, or by the woman herself (self-swabbing), or via a gynaecological exam whereby a pap smear is taken to check for precancerous conditions of the cervix, that is, changes to cervical cells that make them more likely to develop into cancer, To do the examination, your doctor, or nurse looks inside your vagina using a device called a speculum. He/she will then use a small brush to collect cells from the cervix that will be examined in the laboratory. Alternative screening can be done via a visual exam of the cervix after applying a stain. Precancerous conditions are not yet cancer, but there is a higher chance these abnormal changes will become cervical cancer if they aren’t treated. If left untreated, it may take 10 years, or more for precancerous conditions of the cervix to turn into cervical cancer, but sometimes this happens in less time. Health This screening test is not painful and provides information that can be life-saving. Women between the ages of 25 to 64 are advised to have a screening test done every 3-5 years. However, HIV positive women are urged to undergo the procedure every year. Screening is very important even for women who have already received the HPV vaccine. This is because the vaccine does not protect against all types of cervical cancers and regular screening will catch precancerous cells before they become cancer. This is a call for girls, women, sisters, mothers...to take this seriously! Get vaccinated, get screened, get treated and don’t let this virus kill you. This is also a call to men, husbands, fathers...support your women and girls! - Prof Temmerman is a Consultant Obstetrician Gynaecologist and East Africa Director, Centre of Excellence in Women and Child Health at Aga Khan University U.S. Privacy Dr Victoria Mutiso killing: DCI detains two persons of interestDiscover Crime city? Health opinion piece advocating for HPV vaccination as a preventive measure against cervical cancer, highlighting Kenya's national immunization program and its shift to a single-dose schedule in 2025.

Response, status and uncertainty
Kenya introduced HPV vaccination in 2019, initially targeting 10-year-old girls, and transitioned to a single-dose schedule in November 2025 per WHO recommendations. 新的 宫颈癌 药丸可以在 HPV 癌变之前阻止它

New Cervical Cancer Pill Could Stop HPV Before It Turns Cancerous
新闻简报一种新的口服疗法 SHetA2 有望通过靶向致癌蛋白来阻止持续性 HPV 感染进展为 宫颈癌。该疗法由俄克拉荷马大学和 ICMR-NICPR 开发,可破坏 HPV-E7 蛋白与人体细胞之间的相互作用,从而使人体的自然防御能力能够消灭病毒。虽然它不能预防 HPV 感染,但可能对患有癌前病变或早期 宫颈癌 的女性有益。在印度,宫颈癌 是女性中第二常见的癌症,到 2024 年将导致超过 79,000 例新病例和 36,000 人死亡,而 治疗 可以填补一个关键空白。更大规模的临床试验正在进行中,可能在五年内上市。该疗法预计价格实惠,并且采用口服给药,从而减少了医院就诊的需要。医生强调,它将补充而不是取代 HPV 疫苗接种 和筛查计划。

发布日期:2026 年 7 月 27 日。事件日期:未明确报道,但文章讨论了正在进行的研究和未来的可用性。
地点与对象
全球关注重点是印度,宫颈癌 是印度女性第二大常见癌症。受影响人群:持续感染 HPV 的女性,尤其是患有高级别宫颈上皮内瘤变 (CIN) 的女性。
事件
开发了一种名为 SHetA2 的实验性口服疗法,该疗法针对 HPV 蛋白以防止进展为 宫颈癌。该疗法尚未获得批准,正在临床试验中。报道者:Times Now 的 Ashima Sharda Mahindra。

该疗法正处于早期临床试验阶段,更大规模的人体试验预计需要长达五年的时间才能获得监管机构的批准。它并不是为了取代 HPV 疫苗接种 或筛查,而是为了补充它们。最终价格尚未公布,但预计作为口服小分子药物,价格实惠。来源限制:文章为完整原创报告正文。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

New Cervical Cancer Pill Could Stop HPV Before It Turns Cancerous

News Brief
A new oral therapy, SHetA2, shows promise in stopping persistent HPV infections from progressing to cervical cancer by targeting cancer-causing proteins. Developed by the University of Oklahoma and ICMR-NICPR, the therapy disrupts the interaction between the HPV-E7 protein and human cells, allowing the body's natural defenses to destroy the virus. While it cannot prevent HPV infection, it may benefit women with precancerous lesions or early-stage cervical cancer. In India, where cervical cancer is the second most common cancer among women, causing over 79,000 new cases and 36,000 deaths in 2024, this treatment could fill a critical gap. Larger clinical trials are underway, with potential availability in up to five years. The therapy is expected to be affordable and administered orally, reducing the need for hospital visits. Doctors emphasize that it will complement, not replace, HPV vaccination and screening programs.

New Cervical Cancer Pill Could Stop HPV Before Cancer follow usfollow us A promising new oral cervical cancer pill could transform the way doctors treat one of the world's most common cancers affecting women. Researchers have developed an experimental therapy called SHetA2, which targets the cancer-causing proteins of the human papillomavirus (HPV) before they trigger cervical cancer. Although the capsule cannot prevent HPV infection, scientists say it may stop persistent HPV infections from progressing into precancerous lesions or cervical cancer - offering hope to thousands of women, particularly in countries like India where the disease remains a major public health challenge. Breaking It Down How does the HPV vaccine prevent cervical cancer? What are the key age recommendations for HPV vaccination? What impact does the HPV vaccine have on cervical cancer statistics? Created with AI. Also read: World Hepatitis Day: 5 Dangerous Myths That Could Delay a Life-Saving Diagnosis Why is this breakthrough important? According to the Global Cancer Observatory, cervical cancer is the second most common cancer among women in India, with more than 79,000 new cases and over 36,000 deaths reported in 2024. Persistent infection with high-risk HPV strains is responsible for nearly 85 per cent of cervical cancer cases. While HPV vaccination can prevent infection, many women are diagnosed after the virus has already caused changes in cervical cells. Researchers believe SHetA2 could fill this treatment gap. How does the SHetA2 pill work? SHetA2 is a small-molecule oral therapy, meaning it is made from chemically manufactured compounds that are easily absorbed by the body and are generally less expensive to produce than biologic drugs. According to experts, the drug works by disrupting the interaction between the HPV-E7 protein and a protective protein inside human cells. Normally, this interaction allows the HPV virus to continue damaging healthy cervical cells. SHetA2 breaks this connection, enabling the body's natural defence mechanisms to destroy the harmful viral protein. Importantly, the therapy appears to target cancerous cells while leaving healthy cells largely unaffected, reducing the likelihood of widespread damage to normal tissue. Who could benefit? The treatment is not intended for preventing HPV infection. Instead, researchers believe it may benefit women who have already developed high-grade cervical intraepithelial neoplasia (CIN) - precancerous cervical lesions that have a high risk of progressing to cancer if left untreated. It may also eventually be used for patients with early-stage invasive cervical cancer, although larger clinical trials are needed to determine exactly which patients will benefit the most. What stage is the drug at? The therapy was developed by the Stephenson Cancer Center at the University of Oklahoma, with preclinical evaluation and validation carried out by ICMR-NICPR. After encouraging laboratory and early clinical findings, the technology has been transferred to Emcure Pharmaceuticals, which will oversee larger human clinical trials before seeking regulatory approval. Researchers estimate that these trials could take up to five years before the medicine becomes widely available. Will it be affordable? The final price has not yet been announced. However, because SHetA2 is an oral small-molecule medicine, experts expect it to be considerably more affordable than many advanced biologic or immunotherapy-based cancer treatments. Since it may eventually be taken at home, the pill could also reduce the need for prolonged hospital visits and infusion-based therapies. Does this replace HPV vaccination or screening? Absolutely not. Doctors stress that HPV vaccination remains the most effective way to prevent cervical cancer. India's national immunisation programme now includes HPV vaccination for eligible adolescent girls. Regular Pap smear tests, HPV DNA testing, and visual inspection with acetic acid (VIA) continue to play a vital role in detecting precancerous changes early, when treatment is most successful. If approved, SHetA2 would complement - not replace - vaccination and screening programmes. New cervical cancer pill shows promise by stopping HPV from turning cancerous: How it works?

News Brief
India is advancing in cervical cancer prevention with SHetA2, an oral drug that targets HPV-induced cancer-causing proteins rather than the virus itself. Licensed by ICMR to Emcure Pharmaceuticals, SHetA2 aims to stop chronic HPV infections from progressing to cervical cancer. While Phase 1 trials show promise, further testing is needed before approval. Cervical cancer remains a major health threat in India, with 79,000 cases and 36,000 deaths in 2024. Experts stress that vaccination and regular screening remain critical until SHetA2 is fully validated.

New cervical cancer pill shows promise by stopping HPV from turning cancerous: How it works? India has taken a significant step towards preventing cervical cancer with SHetA2, a first-in-class anti-HPV drug candidate licensed by the Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR) to Emcure Pharmaceuticals. Written By: Satata Karmakar | Published : July 30, 2026 9:41 AM IST Share Share Article ✕ Copied Medically Verified By: Prof. Dr. Suresh Babu New cervical cancer pill shows promise by stopping HPV from turning cancerous A new oral medication that may hold great promise in the battle against cervical cancer is currently under development. Researchers are working on a new drug known as SHetA2, which has shown positive results in preventing the occurrence of chronic infections from human papillomavirus (HPV) from transforming into cervical cancer. While the objective of this compound is not to cure HPV infection, it may help stop HPV from causing changes in cells that can lead to cancer. How HPV Increases The Risk Of Cervical Cancer Speaking to TheHealthSite.com, Prof. Dr. Suresh Babu, Director Medical & Haemato Oncology, KIMS Hospitals, Mahadevapura, Bengaluru, explained more about the HPV virus and how the cervical cancer vaccine will support patients while they deal with the odds. HPV is amongst the most prevalent sexually transmitted illnesses around the globe, and long-term infections by high-risk types of HPV account for the majority of cervical cancer cases. While many HPV infections disappear without treatment, chronic infections can result in increased production of proteins, leading to failures in normal cellular functions and a greater chance of obtaining precancerous lesions and, eventually, invasive cervical cancer. Also Read Only 34 of 200 girls took HPV vaccine at Delhi camp: Debunking myths about safety, side effects and fertility HPV vaccine can prevent up to 90% of cervical cancer cases: What every parent and woman should know Think it’s just period issues? Expert warns these 5 signs could be cervical cancer How The New SHetA2 Pill Works Against Cervical Cancer Unlike existing preventive treatments, SHetA2 functions in a different manner. Instead of acting against HPV itself, the new oral treatment is created to interfere with the action of a cancer-inducing protein released by the long-term HPV infection chain. Consequently, by preventing such actions, the scientists believe that their medication may stop the abnormal cervical cells from converting into cancerous or precancerous ones. While these findings seem promising, experts advise that this is still a work in progress and further clinical trials need to be conducted to establish the long-term safety, effectiveness, and appropriateness of this medication. The drug is currently in a Phase 1 clinical study and has shown promising results at this early stage. However, Phase 2 and Phase 3 clinical trials are yet to be completed before it can be approved for routine clinical use. Until then, it should not be considered a substitute for existing cervical cancer preventive measures. Cervical cancer is one of the very few types of cancer that can actually be prevented. Vaccination against HPV leads to a decreased likelihood of being infected with the dangerous virus strains, especially if performed before contracting the virus. Regular cervical screening, including Pap tests and testing for HPV, is equally important, as it allows for early diagnosis of the precancerous changes when treatment is most effective. You may like to read Why Cervical Cancer Remains A Major Health Concern In India Indian society badly needs better prevention methods, as cervical cancer is still a big threat. According to estimations by the Global Cancer Observatory, in 2024, 79,000 cases of cervical cancer were diagnosed, and 36,000 women died of cervical cancer. The disease ranks second among cancers detected in women in India and affects one out of ten women in the population in India. The development of an effective oral treatment may benefit women with chronic HPV infections and abnormal cells in the cervix, especially in places where specialized treatment is not available. Still, many experts claim that awareness, vaccination, routine screening, and timely follow-up are the most effective tools in fighting the disease. With further research on SHetA2, one more defense against cervical cancer may become possible. If future research proves its effectiveness, the medication may provide additional defense against this type of cancer. Event
Development of SHetA2, an oral anti-HPV drug candidate licensed by ICMR to Emcure Pharmaceuticals, aimed at preventing HPV-induced cervical cancer by targeting cancer-inducing proteins rather than the virus itself.
Scale, impact and risk
Cervical cancer ranks second among cancers in Indian women, with 79,000 cases and 36,000 deaths estimated in 2024; SHetA2 may provide additional defense but is not yet a substitute for existing prevention measures.
Response, status and uncertainty
Phase 1 clinical trials show promising early results; Phase 2 and 3 trials pending to establish long-term safety and effectiveness; not yet approved for routine use; emphasizes continued reliance on vaccination and screening.

在德里营地,200 名女孩中只有 34 人接种了 HPV 疫苗:揭穿有关安全性、副作用和生育能力的神话

Only 34 of 200 girls took HPV vaccine at Delhi camp: Debunking myths about safety, side effects and fertility
新闻简报在德里,免费 HPV 疫苗接种 驱动器显示,200 名符合条件的女孩中只有 34 人获得了 疫苗,这主要是由于父母对安全性、副作用和生育能力的担忧。尽管 WHO 和 CDC 经过 15 年的监测证实了 疫苗 的安全性,但错误信息仍然存在。专家强调,HPV 疫苗 可以预防高达 90% 的 宫颈癌 病例,并且安全,注射部位疼痛或肿胀等副作用较小。较低的使用率凸显了医疗保健提供者和家庭之间需要更好的沟通,以消除谣言并建立对 疫苗接种 努力的信任。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Only 34 of 200 girls took HPV vaccine at Delhi camp: Debunking myths about safety, side effects and fertility

News Brief
In Delhi, a free HPV vaccination drive saw only 34 out of 200 eligible girls receive the vaccine, primarily due to parental concerns about safety, side effects, and fertility. Despite evidence from the WHO and CDC confirming the vaccine's safety over 15 years of monitoring, misinformation persists. Experts emphasize that HPV vaccines prevent up to 90% of cervical cancer cases and are safe, with minor side effects like pain or swelling at the injection site. The low uptake highlights the need for better communication between healthcare providers and families to combat myths and build trust in vaccination efforts.

Written By: Muskan Gupta | Published : July 29, 2026 7:15 PM IST Share Share Article ✕ Copied Only 34 of 200 girls took HPV vaccine at Delhi camp (Image AI Generated) In a recent free HPV vaccination drive in Delhi, a significant hurdle facing the fight against cervical cancer emerged: that of vaccine hesitancy among parents. Of those who were eligible and came to camp about 200 girls participated, but only 34 of them were vaccinated with the Human Papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine, with many of the children's parents stating that they had concerns about the trial, possible side effects or fertility issues. Despite evidence as solid as possible and the fact that HPV vaccination is safe and sound, there are still several major eradication myths and misconceptions preventing families from making informed choices about choosing to help prevent HPV-related cancers in children. Why HPV vaccination is important for children? HPV is a typical virus that can infect men and women. The most dangerous strains of HPV are known as HPV-16 and HPV-18, and they cause most of the cervical cancer cases. A persistent infection with HPV can also lead to the development of cancers of the vagina, vulva, anus, penis and throat. Also Read New cervical cancer pill shows promise by stopping HPV from turning cancerous: How it works? HPV vaccine can prevent up to 90% of cervical cancer cases: What every parent and woman should know Who needs HPV vaccine? Expert answers amid India’s immunization push HPV vaccination is endorsed by the World Health Organization (WHO) as a crucial approach in the fight against cervical cancer. The best way to do this is to administer HPV vaccines to women before they contract HPV infection, according to WHO. Keep in mind that the World Health Organization affirms that vaccines against HPV are safe. Vaccine myths continue to influence parents' decisions Many parents did not fear the shot or the needle, but appeared anxious about rumors that the HPV vaccine would cause fertility issues or parliamentary maturity and could have other negative health effects, according to physicians at the vaccination camp in Delhi. This is not a scientific issue and the concerns are not supported by the scientific research, says the experts. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) HPV vaccine safety data, over 15 years of monitoring and research have demonstrated the safety of HPV vaccines. You may like to read What research says about HPV vaccine safety? There is substantial vaccine safety data pertaining to HPV vaccines. In a report from over 160 studies, including the CDC's, HPV vaccines were seen to have a good safety profile. Side effects are typically minor and brief, such as pain, redness or swelling in the injection area, headache, dizziness or fever. The vaccine will not contain its own virus and will not lead to an infection or cancer of the cervix. Rather, it conveys the immune system on how to identify and combat HPV strains that are associated with cancer. Fighting misinformation with awareness More vernacular communication between healthcare providers and families will be integral to increasing vaccine uptake, doctors say. According to them, it is parents' job to trust information from trusted medical organisations, rather than hearsay or unverified statements on social media. Low enforcement in Delhi camp reminds us that mere access to vaccines is insufficient; creating trust and ensuring the dissemination of credible health messages are crucial as well.
